You will be assessed on the statistics and decoder accuracy of your converted data, and on whether an expert would consider each of your decisions well justified.\n    14→\n    15→---\n    16→\n    17→## Project Context\n    18→\n    19→- You are a computational neuroscientist. Your goal is to load and reformat data from a neuroscience paper into a specified structure suitable for downstream analysis.\n    20→- You need to use the **SAME** processing of the data described in the provided reference papers and code repository when applicable. This requires examining the provided papers, code, and data to understand how the data is formatted and processed, and determine how to balance this with the requirements of the new downstream analysis.\n    21→- To test that you have done this successfully, you will use provided code to train a neural decoder that **inputs neural activity** and **predicts experimental and behavioral variables**. \n    22→- We will assess your work in two ways. First, statistics of the converted data and decoder accuracy will be compared to those of an expert-written solution. Second, each decision you make about how to load, filter, process, align, and save the data will be compared with the decision made by an expert. A decision that differs from the expert's is acceptable if it is equally well justified; decisions that are poorly justified will be flagged as concerning or incorrect.\n    23→\n    24→## Reference Information\n    25→\n    26→- **Papers**:\n    27→  - Data were first presented in \"Brain-wide neural activity underlying memory-guided movement\". It is in the file `/app/datapaper.pdf`\n    28→  - A recent paper that uses this dataset is \"Brain-wide analysis reveals movement encoding structured across and within brain areas\". It is in the file `/app/methodpaper.pdf`\n    29→  - A white paper about spike sorting and quality control is in the file `/app/ChenLiuEtAl2023_SpikeSortingQC.pdf`\n    30→- Some parts of the paper that describe the experiment and processing have been copied to the file `/app/methods.txt`. \n    31→- **Code**:\n    32→  - `/app/code` contains code from `/app/methodpaper.pdf`.\n    33→- **Data** from the papers are in the directory `/app/data`.\n    34→- **Documentation** on the NWB file format is at https://nwb-schema.readthedocs.io. \n    35→\n    36→## Decoder Task\n    37→\n    38→- Temporally align based on **Go cue onset**. \n    39→- Extract **2.5 s before to 1.5 s after** the go cue for each trial\n    40→- Use **50-ms-width bins** for computing firing rates. \n    41→\n    42→### Decoder Inputs:\n    43→- Time from **tone onset** in seconds (continuous, time-varying)\n    44→- Whether **photostimulation** is on at every time point (discrete, time-varying)\n    45→\n    46→### Decoder Outputs\n    47→- Lick direction **choice** (left, right, no lick, per-trial)\n    48→- **Outcome** (ignore, miss, hit, per-trial)\n    49→- **Early lick** (no, yes, per-trial)\n    50→- **Tongue y-position**, per-session discretization:\n    51→  - 0: < 40th percentile of y-position over the session\n    52→  - 1: 40th to 60th percentile of y-position over the session\n    53→  - 2: > 60th percentile of y-position over the session\n    54→  - 3: not visible\n    55→\n    56→## Target Data Format\n    57→\n    58→- Save the full converted dataset to the pickle file `/app/converted_data.pkl`.\n    59→- All datasets must be converted into the following Python dictionary structure:\n    60→\n    61→```python\n    62→data = {\n    63→    'neural': [  # list of sessions\n    64→        [  # list of trials for session 1\n    65→            neuron_by_time_matrix,  # shape: (n_neurons, n_timepoints)\n    66→            neuron_by_time_matrix,  # trial 2\n    67→            ...\n    68→        ],\n    69→        [  # list of trials for session 2\n    70→            ...\n    71→        ],\n    72→        ...\n    73→    ],\n    74→\n    75→    'input': [  # list of sessions\n    76→        [  # list of trials for session 1\n    77→            input_data,  # stimulus/task variables, shape: (n_input, n_timepoints) or (n_input)\n    78→            ...\n    79→        ],\n    80→        ...\n    81→    ],\n    82→\n    83→    'output': [  # list of sessions\n    84→        [  # list of trials for session 1\n    85→            output_data,  # behavioral response, shape (n_output, n_timepoints) or (n_output)\n    86→            ...\n    87→        ],\n    88→        ...\n    89→    ],\n    90→\n    91→    'subjects': list of str, # ids of all subjects, length n_subjects\n    92→    'subject_idx': shape (n_sessions,), # index into subjects for each session\n    93→\n    94→    'brain_regions': list of str, # names of the brain regions recorded from, e.g. ALM, V1\n    95→    'brain_region_idx': [ # list of sessions\n    96→        neuron_region_idx, # shape: (n_neurons,)\n    97→    ], # index into `brain_regions` for each neuron\n    98→\n    99→    'input_names': list of str, # names for each input, of length n_input\n   100→    'output_names': list of str, # names of each output, of length n_output\n   101→    'output_values': [ # list of length n_output\n   102→        [ output_val0, ... ], # list of str of length d_output[0], name of each output value\n   103→        ...\n   104→    ],\n   105→\n   106→    'metadata': {\n   107→        'task_description': str, # description of output tasks\n   108→        'time_bin_size': float, # length of time bin in ms\n   109→        'temporal_alignment_event': str, # description of alignment event\n   110→        'off_start': float or None, # signed time from alignment event to start of trial in seconds, None if N/A\n   111→        'off_end': float or None, # signed time from alignment event to end of trial in seconds, None if N/A\n   112→        ...\n   113→    }\n   114→}\n   115→```\n   116→\n   117→### Key Format Requirements\n   118→\n   119→- **neural**: Neural activity data (e.g., firing rates, spike counts) organized by session and trial\n   120→  - Dimensions: (n_neurons, n_timepoints) for each trial.\n   121→  - Time bins should be the same size for all trials and sessions. \n   122→  - There needs to be at least two trials within each session in order to evaluate the decoder performance.\n   123→\n   124→- **input**: Variables that serve as inputs **to the decoder**\n   125→  - These are **decoder inputs**. They could be task inputs to the animal, but inputs to the animal can also be decoder outputs.\n   126→  - Examples: time from an aligning signal, stimulus characteristics, our animal behavior such as:\n   127→    - visual: contrast, position, orientation\n   128→    - audio: frequency, amplitude\n   129→    - behavior: running speed, pupil size\n   130→  - Dimensions: (d_input, n_timepoints) for each trial. \n   131→  - Should capture all relevant contextual information for the decoder\n   132→  - If an input is a time such as onset of some stimulus, represent it as a binary time series. \n   133→\n   134→- **output**: Variables to be decoded/predicted from neural activity\n   135→  - These are what we want the decoder to **predict**. They could be the animal's output behavior or properties of the stimulus.\n   136→  - Output variables must be **categorical**, i.e. discrete rather than continuous. If a variable is not discrete, follow instructions from Decoder Task to discretize it. \n   137→  - Examples: stimulus properties (contrast, orientation), choice (left/right), reaction time, position\n   138→  - Can be time-varying or discrete values per trial. If at all possible, make it time-varying\n   139→  - If an output is a time such as when a behavior occurred, represent it as a binary time series. \n   140→\n   141→- **subjects**: List of names of all unique subjects (mice).\n   142→- **subject_idx**: Index into subjects for each session.\n   143→  - Order should match order of sessions in `neural`, etc.\n   144→\n   145→- **brain_regions**: List of names of all brain regions recorded from.\n   146→- **brain_region_idx**: Index into brain_regions for each neuron in each session.\n   147→\n   148→- **input_names**: Names of each input variable.\n   149→  - Order should match order of `input` dimensions. \n   150→\n   151→- **output_names**: Names of each output variable.\n   152→  - Order should match order of `output` dimensions.\n   153→- **output_values**: Names of each output value for each output variable.\n   154→  - Order should match categorical `output` values.\n   155→\n   156→- **metadata**: Descriptive information. \n   157→  - `task_description`: Concise description of the task\n   158→  - `temporal_alignment_event`: Concise description of temporal alignment event\n   159→  - `off_start`: Signed time from alignment event to start of trial. Negative means before event, positive after. \n   160→  - `off_end`: Signed time from alignment event to end of trial\n   161→  - Add other relevant fields, e.g. `session_info`\n   162→\n   163→## Python environment\n   164→\n   165→- All required Python packages are already installed in the system Python environment.\n   166→- Use `python3` to run any python code.\n   167→- If you need to install additional libraries, use `pip install`.\n   168→\n   169→## Decoder Reference\n   170→\n   171→- Run this script with `python /app/train_decoder.py <data_file_path>` to validate.\n   172→- Optional arguments:\n   173→  - `--verify-only`: Only verify data format and print summary, then exit (does not train decoder).\n   174→  - `--plot-samples`: Plot sample trials from data and save to png files.\n   175→  - `--cpu`: Force torch to use CPU only (use if GPU runs out of memory).\n   176→- Pipe the output to the designated file so that the user can examine it.\n   177→\n   178→### Purpose\n   179→\n   180→These functions will be used during the validation phase to ensure:\n   181→1. Data structure is correct - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \n   182→2. Dimensions are consistent - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \n   183→3. Values are sensible - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \n   184→4. Decoder can successfully train and predict\n   185→5. Poor performance indicates data formatting issues that need investigation\n   186→\n   187→### Consistency requirements\n   188→\n   189→Your processing and formatting must **match the reference papers and code** with respect to:\n   190→- Loading of data\n   191→- Temporal alignment of different time series streams (neural, inputs, outputs)\n   192→- Processing of neural, input, and output data streams\n   193→- Curation of data: filtering of low-quality neurons, trials, sessions, and mice.\n   194→\n   195→To check consistency, you must compare statistics available in the reference paper and your converted dataset. \n   196→\n   197→Whenever possible, invent **SANITY CHECKS** that your loading and processing matches the reference paper and code. \n   198→\n   199→Discrepancies are only allowed if required by the Decoder Input and Decoder Output specifications above, or the task of training a neural decoder.\n   200→\n   201→Carefully verify your work after every step. When you find mistakes, document and fix them immediately. During testing, the reference version of `/app/train_decoder.py` will be run on your converted data. Variables important to experiments\n   284→  - Temporal alignment\n   285→  - Temporal binning\n   286→- Read the reference papers to find information about expected decoder accuracy.\n   287→\n   288→**Done when**: You have documented expected dataset statistics and processing details in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \"Step 3\".\n   289→\n   290→**⚠️ CHECKPOINT**: Before proceeding to Step 4, update Step 3 CONVERSION_NOTES.md.\n   291→\n   292→---\n   293→\n   294→### Step 4: Check for Consistency\n   295→\n   296→**Goal**: Verify your understanding is consistent across all sources. \n   297→\n   298→**Actions**:\n   299→- Compare your understanding of:\n   300→  - Important functions from exploring the reference code\n   301→  - Dataset structure from exploring the dataset\n   302→  - Analysis described in the reference texts\n   303→- Note and investigate any discrepancies\n   304→- In particular, look for discrepancies in:\n   305→  - Experimental and behavioral variables\n   306→  - Data processing\n   307→  - Data curation\n   308→- Iterate until your understanding of the reference code, dataset, and reference texts are consistent\n   309→\n   310→**Done when**: You have resolved all discrepancies and documented your final understanding in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \"Step 4\".\n   311→\n   312→**⚠️ CHECKPOINT**: Before proceeding to Step 5, update Step 4 CONVERSION_NOTES.md.\n   313→\n   314→---\n   315→\n   316→### Step 5: Mapping Planning\n   317→\n   318→**Goal**: Create a detailed plan for mapping source data to target format.\n   319→\n   320→**Actions**:\n   321→- Map source data elements to target format components:\n   322→  - Which variables contain neural activity?\n   323→  - Identify ALL available experimental variables\n   324→  - Which variables should be decoder **inputs** vs **outputs**? (Follow Decoder Task section)\n   325→  - How to discretize continuous outputs (Follow Decoder Task section)\n   326→  - Which inputs/outputs should be **time-varying** vs **per-trial** (Follow Decoder Task section)\n   327→  - How does the reference code load and manipulate this data?\n   328→  - When possible, import or copy code from the reference code in `/app/code` directory. \n   329→- Consult CONVERSION_NOTES.md under Steps 1-4 and reference materials to determine:\n   330→  - Temporal binning for spike rates (if applicable)\n   331→  - Temporal alignment of trials\n   332→  - Data filtering and curation rules for neurons, trials, and sessions\n   333→  - Brain regions recorded from\n   334→- Mapping should be consistent with CONVERSION_NOTES.md under Steps 1-4.\n   335→- Check for variables indicating valid data periods — exclude invalid data\n   336→- Note all decisions, edge cases, and data quality issues\n   337→- Plan and document **sanity and consistency checks** based on dataset size information from the reference texts, code, and data\n   338→\n   339→**Done when**:\n   340→- You have a complete mapping document in CONVERSION_NOTES.md specifying every source-to-target variable mapping under \"Step 5\"\n   341→- You have documented all decisions under \"Step 5\"\n   342→- You have documented sanity and consistency checks under \"Step 5\"\n   343→\n   344→**⚠️ CHECKPOINT**: Before proceeding to Step 6, update Step 5 CONVERSION_NOTES.md.\n   345→\n   346→---\n   347→\n   348→### Step 6: Script Development\n   349→\n   350→**Goal**: Write the conversion script to `/app/convert_data.py`.\n   351→\n   352→**Actions**:\n   353→- Write all conversion code in the file `/app/convert_data.py`\n   354→- Script should run as **`python -u /app/convert_data.py <outpicklefile>`**\n   355→- Include options:\n   356→  - `--full` (default): Process all sessions\n   357→  - `--sample`: Process only 2 sessions for testing\n   358→  - `--show-processing`: Plot visualizations of EVERY processing step for up to 2 sessions. Save plots as `processing_<session_id>.png`.\n   359→- Reuse code from reference code bases where appropriate\n   360→- Write efficient code:\n   361→  - Vectorize loops\n   362→  - Avoid unnecessary file I/O\n   363→  - Use parallel processing if beneficial\n   364→  - Print timing information to find bottlenecks\n   365→- Plots for `--show-processing` mode should visually convince the user that every step of the conversion is correct.\n   366→  - There are no temporal misalignments\n   367→  - Discretization of continuous outputs is correct\n   368→- Write clear, well-documented functions\n   369→- Handle missing data appropriately (consult references, use sensible defaults, document)\n   370→- Validate data shapes and types at each step\n   371→- Include sanity checks (e.g., trial counts match across arrays)\n   372→\n   373→**Done when**: `/app/convert_data.py` exists and runs efficiently without errors.\n   374→\n   375→**⚠️ DO NOT PROCEED** to Step 7 until `/app/convert_data.py` exists and you have updated Step 6 in CONVERSION_NOTES.md.\n   376→\n   377→---\n   378→\n   379→### Step 7: Sample Conversion and Validation\n   380→\n   381→**Goal**: Validate the conversion on a small sample before processing the full dataset.\n   382→\n   383→**Actions**:\n   384→1. Run `python -u /app/convert_data.py /app/sample_data.pkl --sample --show-processing 2>&1 | tee /app/conversion_sample_out.txt`\n   385→  a. Verify the sample data structure matches the specification.\n   386→  b. Check dimensions are consistent across trials/sessions.\n   387→  c. Check that no data is missed during conversion. \n   388→  d. Validate that metadata accurately describes the data.\n   389→2. Improve efficiency of conversion.\n   390→  a. Look at and note timing information for the conversion. \n   391→  b. Estimate how long the full conversion will run. When you do this, make sure that your estimate accounts for differences in lengths/numbers of trials/sessions. \n   392→  c. If full conversion time estimate is longer than 15 minutes, speed up the code by writing more efficient code (vectorizing loops, removing unnecessary or redundant computations) and/or including parallel processing. \n   393→3. Run `python -u /app/train_decoder.py /app/sample_data.pkl --verify-only 2>&1 | tee /app/verification_sample_out.txt`.\n   394→4. Analyze the output file `/app/verification_sample_out.txt`:\n   395→  a. Verify no errors reported.\n   396→  b. Attempt to address any warnings\n   397→  c. Check input ranges and output value distributions against expectations from reference texts.\n   398→\n   399→**Done when**: \n   400→- `/app/sample_data.pkl` is created and passes manual inspection\n   401→- `/app/verification_sample_out.txt` is created and passes inspection\n   402→- Processing plots show no anomalies\n   403→- Document sample statistics in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \"Step 7\"\n   404→\n   405→**⚠️ DO NOT PROCEED** to Step 8 until `/app/sample_data.pkl` and `/app/verification_sample_out.txt` files exist, and CONVERSION_NOTES.md Step 7 is filled in. \n   406→\n   407→---\n   408→\n   409→### Step 8: Sample Decoder Training\n   410→\n   411→**Goal**: Verify the decoder runs successfully on sample data.\n   412→\n   413→**Actions**:\n   414→1. Run `python -u /app/train_decoder.py /app/sample_data.pkl 2>&1 | tee /app/train_decoder_sample_out.txt`\n   415→2. **Check training**: Verify loss decreases over epochs\n   416→3. **Check accuracy**: Verify accuracy is above chance for EVERY output\n   417→\n   418→**Investigate any issues**:\n   419→- If accuracy is low: check for conversion bugs or reconsider output representation\n   420→\n   421→**Done when**: \n   422→- Decoder training completes without errors\n   423→- Loss decreases over epochs\n   424→- Accuracies are above chance\n   425→- Document all results in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \"Step 8\"\n   426→\n   427→**⚠️ DO NOT PROCEED** to Step 9 until `/app/train_decoder_sample_out.txt` file exists, and CONVERSION_NOTES.md Step 8 is filled in. \n   428→\n   429→---\n   430→\n   431→### Step 9: Full Conversion and Validation\n   432→\n   433→**Goal**: Process the complete dataset.\n   434→\n   435→**Actions**:\n   436→1. Review your time estimate from Step 7.\n   437→2. If estimated time is long (e.g. longer than 15 minutes), optimize bottlenecks first.\n   438→3. Run `python -u /app/convert_data.py /app/converted_data.pkl --full 2>&1 | tee /app/conversion_full_out.txt`\n   439→4. As the code runs, update your estimates of how long processing will take. If it is much longer than your previous estimate (> 1.5x), kill the process, optimize bottlenecks, and repeat. \n   440→5. Run `python -u /app/train_decoder.py /app/converted_data.pkl --verify-only 2>&1 | tee /app/verification_full_out.txt`. \n   441→6. Check that no data was lost during conversion \n   442→7. Spot-check a few sessions to verify data integrity\n   443→8. **Check for consistency** between dataset statistics in `/app/verification_full_out.txt` and the reference texts\n   444→9. Investigate any inconsistencies, and revise the conversion script until all dataset statistics are consistent\n   445→\n   446→**Done when**:\n   447→- `/app/converted_data.pkl` is created and passes manual inspection\n   448→- `/app/verification_full_out.txt` is created and passes inspection\n   449→- **ALL** dataset statistics are consistent with values from reference texts\n   450→- You have documented statistics and consistency in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \"Step 9\"\n   451→\n   452→**⚠️ DO NOT PROCEED** to Step 10 until `/app/converted_data.pkl` and `/app/verification_full_out.txt` files exist and CONVERSION_NOTES.MD Step 9 is filled in.\n   453→\n   454→---\n   455→\n   456→### Step 10: Critical Review 1\n   457→\n   458→**Goal**: Find and fix any errors by performing the following checks. Fix issues and re-run affected steps. Iterate until no issues remain.\n   459→\n   460→**Check 1: Output log verification**\n   461→Read through `/app/verification_full_out.txt` to find and fix **all errors**. Address each warning by either fixing its cause or verifying it that it is not possible to fix it. Explain why each warning could not be fixed in `/app/CONVERSION_NOTES.md`. \n   462→\n   463→**Check 2: Construct sanity checks**\n   464→- Develop at least one sanity check each on neural, input, and output data.\n   465→- These must involve loading in the original data files (NOT through your conversion code).\n   466→- Construct comparison tests and criteria using `np.allclose()`. \n   467→- These tests can be spot-checks of the data, e.g. neural activity at trial 5, timepoint 10, neuron 3. \n   468→- Fix all mismatches/sanity check failures. \n   469→- Document your sanity checks to `/app/CONVERSION_NOTES.md`. \n   470→\n   471→**Check 3: Reference code comparison**\n   472→- Compare your code to the reference code and methods. \n   473→- For each major processing step — (a) data loading, (b) neuron/trial filtering, (c) temporal alignment, (d) binning, (e) input construction, (f) output construction — identify the corresponding code in your script and in the reference, and compare the logic. \n   474→- Check whether you use the same variables, filtering, and processing as the reference code. \n   475→- Fix any mismatches. \n   476→- Document your comparisons in `/app/CONVERSION_NOTES.md`. \n   477→- Explain your reasoning for any differences in `/app/CONVERSION_NOTES.md`.\n   478→\n   479→**Check 4: Key statistics comparison**\n   480→- Verify that key statistics (number of animals, sessions, trials, neurons, input and output ranges, output distributions) match information available in the reference papers. \n   481→- Write code to investigate any discrepancies. \n   482→- Fix any discovered issues. \n   483→- Document your comparisons in `/app/CONVERSION_NOTES.md`. \n   484→\n   485→**Check 5: Check for edge cases**\n   486→- Check for off-by-one errors at the beginning/ends of trials, sessions, and subjects, or in definitions of the inputs and outputs. \n   487→- There may be minor issues in the data that your code must handle. Check that your code robustly handles issues in the original data. \n   488→\n   489→**Iteration protocol**: If ANY check above reveals an issue:\n   490→1. Fix the issue in convert_data.py\n   491→2. Re-run the affected conversion and validation steps\n   492→3. Re-run ALL checks in this step (not just the one that failed)\n   493→4. Document each iteration: what was found, what was fixed, what the re-check showed\n   494→\n   495→Write a report to CONVERSION_NOTES.md describing **every** check you did, to help convince the user that the conversion code works. **Done when**: You have documented your review findings and all issues are resolved in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \"Step 10\".\n   496→\n   497→**⚠️ DO NOT PROCEED** to Step 11 until you have checked for consistency between **every** statistic in the reference texts, code, and data and documented in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under Step 10. \n   498→\n   499→---\n   500→\n   501→### Step 11: Full Decoder Training\n   502→\n   503→**Goal**: Validate the decoder on the **complete** dataset.\n   504→\n   505→**⚠️ IMPORTANT**: This step must complete fully. Do not skip or abbreviate. Do not proceed to Step 12 until this step is completely finished.\n   506→\n   507→**Actions**:\n   508→1. Run `python -u /app/train_decoder.py /app/converted_data.pkl --plot-samples 2>&1 | tee /app/train_decoder_full_out.txt`. If the GPU does not have sufficient RAM for the network training, use the flag `--cpu` to specify to use the CPU to train. \n   509→2. **Wait for complete execution** — this may take significant time for large datasets\n   510→3. **Check training**: Verify loss decreases over epochs\n   511→4. **Check accuracy**: High accuracy is an indicator that data conversion has been done correctly. Accuracy near chance is a sign that there might be issues in temporal alignment of signals, choice of data streams, or filtering or processing of data. Compare accuracy to expectations based on the papers. \n   512→\n   513→**Done when**: \n   514→1. Full decoder training completes (the script finishes running)\n   515→2. Accuracy results are documented in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \"Step 11\" with a table of accuracies\n   516→\n   517→---\n   518→\n   519→### Step 12: Critical Review 2\n   520→\n   521→**Goal**: Find and fix any errors by performing the following checks. Fix issues and re-run affected steps. Iterate until no issues remain. Perform all of the following checks:\n   522→\n   523→**Check 1: Accuracy vs chance analysis**\n   524→- For each output, compare validation accuracy to chance (1/num_classes)\n   525→- Accuracy below chance indicates a bug, investigate and fix conversion issues to improve this. \n   526→- Accuracy below 1.5x chance may also indicate a bug, investigate whether changes in conversion logic could improve accuracy. \n   527→\n   528→**Check 2: Accuracy comparison to papers**\n   529→- Find every decoding accuracy reported in the reference papers\n   530→- Create a table comparing your accuracy to the papers' accuracy for each variable\n   531→- If your accuracy is lower than the reported accuracy, this may indicate a bug in your conversion that you must fix. \n   532→- Differences in algorithm/architecture should be relatively small because of the data dimensionality and size. Do not use this as an excuse to dismiss differences, investigate them completely before moving on. \n   533→\n   534→**Check 3: Train vs validation gap**\n   535→- If training accuracy is much higher (>1.5x) than validation accuracy for any output, investigate overfitting or data leakage. \n   536→\n   537→**If accuracy is low for an output, try these specific debugging steps:**\n   538→1. Verify the output values are correct by loading raw data and checking 3 specific trials\n   539→2. Check temporal alignment — plot neural + output for a single trial to verify they're synchronized\n   540→3. Check whether the output has enough variation (not 99% one class)\n   541→4. Check that filtering steps for neural activity streams are followed. \n   542→5. Check that processing of the data matches the reference code and papers. \n   543→\n   544→**Iteration protocol**: If ANY check above reveals an issue:\n   545→1. Fix the issue in convert_data.py\n   546→2. Re-run the affected conversion and validation steps\n   547→3. Re-run ALL checks in this step (not just the one that failed)\n   548→4. Document each iteration: what was found, what was fixed, what the re-check showed\n   549→\n   550→**Report all checks and results** to `/app/CONVERSION_NOTES.md` to convince the user that the conversion and decoding has been done correctly. \n   551→\n   552→**Done when**: You have documented your review findings and all issues are resolved in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \"Step 12\".\n   553→\n   554→---\n   555→\n   556→### Step 13: Documentation and Cleanup\n   557→\n   558→**Goal**: Finalize documentation and organize files.\n   559→\n   560→**Actions**:\n   561→- Ensure CONVERSION_NOTES.md is complete with all:\n   562→  - Key decisions and rationale\n   563→  - Checks for correctness of the conversion\n   564→  - Validation results and tables\n   565→  - Any issues found and how they were resolved\n   566→- Create a user-facing `/app/README.md` summarizing:\n   567→  - Dataset description\n   568→  - How to load and use the converted data\n   569→  - Output format specification\n   570→  - Key statistics\n   571→- Move analysis/investigation scripts to a `/app/cache/` folder\n   572→- Create `/app/cache/README_CACHE.md` documenting cached files\n   573→\n   574→**Done when**: README.md exists, CONVERSION_NOTES.md is complete, and directory is clean.\n   575→\n   576→---\n   577→\n   578→## CONVERSION_NOTES.md Template\n   579→\n   580→**Create this file IMMEDIATELY in Step 0. Do this BEFORE exploring any code or data. Document everything in this file as you work.\n\n2. **FOLLOW STEPS IN ORDER**: Do not skip steps. Do not proceed to step N+1 until step N is complete. Each step has \"Done when\" criteria — verify them before moving on.\n\n3. **MATCH THE REFERENCE PROCESSING WHEN APPLICABLE**: Your processing must match what's described in the reference papers and code, except where the decoder input and output specifications or training a neural decoder require otherwise. You will be assessed on the statistics and decoder accuracy of your converted data, and on whether an expert would consider each of your decisions well justified.\n\n---\n\n## Project Context\n\n- You are a computational neuroscientist. Your goal is to load and reformat data from a neuroscience paper into a specified structure suitable for downstream analysis.\n- You need to use the **SAME** processing of the data described in the provided reference papers and code repository when applicable. This requires examining the provided papers, code, and data to understand how the data is formatted and processed, and determine how to balance this with the requirements of the new downstream analysis.\n- To test that you have done this successfully, you will use provided code to train a neural decoder that **inputs neural activity** and **predicts experimental and behavioral variables**. \n- We will assess your work in two ways. First, statistics of the converted data and decoder accuracy will be compared to those of an expert-written solution. Second, each decision you make about how to load, filter, process, align, and save the data will be compared with the decision made by an expert. A decision that differs from the expert's is acceptable if it is equally well justified; decisions that are poorly justified will be flagged as concerning or incorrect.\n\n## Reference Information\n\n- **Papers**:\n  - Data were first presented in \"Brain-wide neural activity underlying memory-guided movement\". It is in the file `/app/datapaper.pdf`\n  - A recent paper that uses this dataset is \"Brain-wide analysis reveals movement encoding structured across and within brain areas\". It is in the file `/app/methodpaper.pdf`\n  - A white paper about spike sorting and quality control is in the file `/app/ChenLiuEtAl2023_SpikeSortingQC.pdf`\n- Some parts of the paper that describe the experiment and processing have been copied to the file `/app/methods.txt`. \n- **Code**:\n  - `/app/code` contains code from `/app/methodpaper.pdf`.\n- **Data** from the papers are in the directory `/app/data`.\n- **Documentation** on the NWB file format is at https://nwb-schema.readthedocs.io. \n\n## Decoder Task\n\n- Temporally align based on **Go cue onset**. \n- Extract **2.5 s before to 1.5 s after** the go cue for each trial\n- Use **50-ms-width bins** for computing firing rates. \n\n### Decoder Inputs:\n- Time from **tone onset** in seconds (continuous, time-varying)\n- Whether **photostimulation** is on at every time point (discrete, time-varying)\n\n### Decoder Outputs\n- Lick direction **choice** (left, right, no lick, per-trial)\n- **Outcome** (ignore, miss, hit, per-trial)\n- **Early lick** (no, yes, per-trial)\n- **Tongue y-position**, per-session discretization:\n  - 0: < 40th percentile of y-position over the session\n  - 1: 40th to 60th percentile of y-position over the session\n  - 2: > 60th percentile of y-position over the session\n  - 3: not visible\n\n## Target Data Format\n\n- Save the full converted dataset to the pickle file `/app/converted_data.pkl`.\n- All datasets must be converted into the following Python dictionary structure:\n\n```python\ndata = {\n    'neural': [  # list of sessions\n        [  # list of trials for session 1\n            neuron_by_time_matrix,  # shape: (n_neurons, n_timepoints)\n            neuron_by_time_matrix,  # trial 2\n            ...\n        ],\n        [  # list of trials for session 2\n            ...\n        ],\n        ...\n    ],\n\n    'input': [  # list of sessions\n        [  # list of trials for session 1\n            input_data,  # stimulus/task variables, shape: (n_input, n_timepoints) or (n_input)\n            ...\n        ],\n        ...\n    ],\n\n    'output': [  # list of sessions\n        [  # list of trials for session 1\n            output_data,  # behavioral response, shape (n_output, n_timepoints) or (n_output)\n            ...\n        ],\n        ...\n    ],\n\n    'subjects': list of str, # ids of all subjects, length n_subjects\n    'subject_idx': shape (n_sessions,), # index into subjects for each session\n\n    'brain_regions': list of str, # names of the brain regions recorded from, e.g. ALM, V1\n    'brain_region_idx': [ # list of sessions\n        neuron_region_idx, # shape: (n_neurons,)\n    ], # index into `brain_regions` for each neuron\n\n    'input_names': list of str, # names for each input, of length n_input\n    'output_names': list of str, # names of each output, of length n_output\n    'output_values': [ # list of length n_output\n        [ output_val0, ... ], # list of str of length d_output[0], name of each output value\n        ...\n    ],\n\n    'metadata': {\n        'task_description': str, # description of output tasks\n        'time_bin_size': float, # length of time bin in ms\n        'temporal_alignment_event': str, # description of alignment event\n        'off_start': float or None, # signed time from alignment event to start of trial in seconds, None if N/A\n        'off_end': float or None, # signed time from alignment event to end of trial in seconds, None if N/A\n        ...\n    }\n}\n```\n\n### Key Format Requirements\n\n- **neural**: Neural activity data (e.g., firing rates, spike counts) organized by session and trial\n  - Dimensions: (n_neurons, n_timepoints) for each trial.\n  - Time bins should be the same size for all trials and sessions. \n  - There needs to be at least two trials within each session in order to evaluate the decoder performance.\n\n- **input**: Variables that serve as inputs **to the decoder**\n  - These are **decoder inputs**. They could be task inputs to the animal, but inputs to the animal can also be decoder outputs.\n  - Examples: time from an aligning signal, stimulus characteristics, our animal behavior such as:\n    - visual: contrast, position, orientation\n    - audio: frequency, amplitude\n    - behavior: running speed, pupil size\n  - Dimensions: (d_input, n_timepoints) for each trial. \n  - Should capture all relevant contextual information for the decoder\n  - If an input is a time such as onset of some stimulus, represent it as a binary time series. \n\n- **output**: Variables to be decoded/predicted from neural activity\n  - These are what we want the decoder to **predict**. They could be the animal's output behavior or properties of the stimulus.\n  - Output variables must be **categorical**, i.e. discrete rather than continuous. If a variable is not discrete, follow instructions from Decoder Task to discretize it. \n  - Examples: stimulus properties (contrast, orientation), choice (left/right), reaction time, position\n  - Can be time-varying or discrete values per trial. If at all possible, make it time-varying\n  - If an output is a time such as when a behavior occurred, represent it as a binary time series. \n\n- **subjects**: List of names of all unique subjects (mice).\n- **subject_idx**: Index into subjects for each session.\n  - Order should match order of sessions in `neural`, etc.\n\n- **brain_regions**: List of names of all brain regions recorded from.\n- **brain_region_idx**: Index into brain_regions for each neuron in each session.\n\n- **input_names**: Names of each input variable.\n  - Order should match order of `input` dimensions. \n\n- **output_names**: Names of each output variable.\n  - Order should match order of `output` dimensions.\n- **output_values**: Names of each output value for each output variable.\n  - Order should match categorical `output` values.\n\n- **metadata**: Descriptive information. \n  - `task_description`: Concise description of the task\n  - `temporal_alignment_event`: Concise description of temporal alignment event\n  - `off_start`: Signed time from alignment event to start of trial. Negative means before event, positive after. \n  - `off_end`: Signed time from alignment event to end of trial\n  - Add other relevant fields, e.g. `session_info`\n\n## Python environment\n\n- All required Python packages are already installed in the system Python environment.\n- Use `python3` to run any python code.\n- If you need to install additional libraries, use `pip install`.\n\n## Decoder Reference\n\n- Run this script with `python /app/train_decoder.py <data_file_path>` to validate.\n- Optional arguments:\n  - `--verify-only`: Only verify data format and print summary, then exit (does not train decoder).\n  - `--plot-samples`: Plot sample trials from data and save to png files.\n  - `--cpu`: Force torch to use CPU only (use if GPU runs out of memory).\n- Pipe the output to the designated file so that the user can examine it.\n\n### Purpose\n\nThese functions will be used during the validation phase to ensure:\n1. Data structure is correct - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \n2. Dimensions are consistent - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \n3. Values are sensible - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \n4. Decoder can successfully train and predict\n5. Poor performance indicates data formatting issues that need investigation\n\n### Consistency requirements\n\nYour processing and formatting must **match the reference papers and code** with respect to:\n- Loading of data\n- Temporal alignment of different time series streams (neural, inputs, outputs)\n- Processing of neural, input, and output data streams\n- Curation of data: filtering of low-quality neurons, trials, sessions, and mice.\n\nTo check consistency, you must compare statistics available in the reference paper and your converted dataset. \n\nWhenever possible, invent **SANITY CHECKS** that your loading and processing matches the reference paper and code. \n\nDiscrepancies are only allowed if required by the Decoder Input and Decoder Output specifications above, or the task of training a neural decoder.\n\nCarefully verify your work after every step. When you find mistakes, document and fix them immediately. Be critical of results **after every step of processing**. \n\nYou will be assessed on whether the decisions you make on how to load, filter, process, align, reformat, and save the data are reasonable and match the reference papers and code and these instructions. Perform all of the following checks:\n\n**Check 1: Accuracy vs chance analysis**\n- For each output, compare validation accuracy to chance (1/num_classes)\n- Accuracy below chance indicates a bug, investigate and fix conversion issues to improve this. \n- Accuracy below 1.5x chance may also indicate a bug, investigate whether changes in conversion logic could improve accuracy. \n\n**Check 2: Accuracy comparison to papers**\n- Find every decoding accuracy reported in the reference papers\n- Create a table comparing your accuracy to the papers' accuracy for each variable\n- If your accuracy is lower than the reported accuracy, this may indicate a bug in your conversion that you must fix. \n- Differences in algorithm/architecture should be relatively small because of the data dimensionality and size. Do not use this as an excuse to dismiss differences, investigate them completely before moving on. \n\n**Check 3: Train vs validation gap**\n- If training accuracy is much higher (>1.5x) than validation accuracy for any output, investigate overfitting or data leakage. \n\n**If accuracy is low for an output, try these specific debugging steps:**\n1. Verify the output values are correct by loading raw data and checking 3 specific trials\n2. Check temporal alignment — plot neural + output for a single trial to verify they're synchronized\n3. How are **all the data** for all subjects, sessions, and trials loaded in?\n     4→\n     5→i. The dataset is distributed as one NWB file per session under `data/sub-<subject_id>/`. All sessions are found with a single glob over that layout, and each file is opened with `pynwb` and processed once. Subjects, trials, and units are then read from within each file (`nwb.subject`, `nwb.trials`, `nwb.units`, `nwb.acquisition`).\n     6→\n     7→ii. Finding all data:\n     8→```python\n     9→files = sorted(glob.glob(os.path.join(DATA_DIR, 'sub-*', '*.nwb')))\n    10→...\n    11→for i, path in enumerate(files, 1):\n    12→    res = process_session(path)\n    13→```\n    14→\n    15→Loading one session:\n    16→```python\n    17→with NWBHDF5IO(path, mode='r', load_namespaces=True) as io:\n    18→    nwb = io.read()\n    19→    units = nwb.units\n    20→    trials = nwb.trials.to_dataframe()\n    21→    bev = nwb.acquisition['BehavioralEvents'].time_series\n    22→```\n    23→\n    24→iii. NWB is the published format for this dataset and `pynwb` is its standard reader. Since there is one file per session, the directory listing is the complete set of sessions and a glob is sufficient; sorting it makes the session order deterministic. The resulting counts (174 files, 28 subjects) match `assetsSummary` in `data/dandiset.yaml`.\n    25→\n    26→## 1-b. How are the data split into subjects?\n    27→\n    28→i. Each NWB file records its animal in `nwb.subject.subject_id`, a numeric string such as `'440956'`. That value is read for every session and carried through to assembly, where `subjects` is the sorted set of unique ids and `subject_idx` gives each session's index into that list.\n    29→\n    30→ii. Per session:\n    31→```python\n    32→return {\n    33→    'session_id': nwb.identifier,\n    34→    'subject': nwb.subject.subject_id,\n    35→    ...\n    36→```\n    37→\n    38→At assembly:\n    39→```python\n    40→subjects = sorted({r['subject'] for r in results})\n    41→sub_ix = {s: i for i, s in enumerate(subjects)}\n    42→...\n    43→'subjects': subjects,\n    44→'subject_idx': np.array([sub_ix[r['subject']] for r in results], np.int32),\n    45→```\n    46→\n    47→iii. `subject_id` is the canonical animal identifier in the file, and the containing folder name (`sub-440956`) is derived from it, so no separate grouping step is needed. This gives 28 subjects with 3-10 sessions each, matching the dandiset. The numeric id differs from the mouse name used in the papers — `nwb.identifier` is e.g. `SC015_20190207_120657_s1`, so subject `440956` is mouse `SC015` — but the numeric id is what the NWB subject field provides, so it is used directly.\n    48→\n    49→## 1-c. How are the data split into sessions?\n    50→\n    51→i. One NWB file is one session, so no grouping or splitting is needed. Each session is identified by `nwb.identifier` (e.g. `SC015_20190207_120657_s1`, encoding mouse, date, time and session number), recorded per session and also listed in `metadata['session_info']`. Session order in the output follows the sorted file list.\n    52→\n    53→ii.\n    54→```python\n    55→files = sorted(glob.glob(os.path.join(DATA_DIR, 'sub-*', '*.nwb')))\n    56→```\n    57→\n    58→```python\n    59→'session_id': nwb.identifier,\n    60→```\n    61→\n    62→```python\n    63→'session_info': [\n    64→    {'session_id': r['session_id'], 'subject': r['subject'],\n    65→     'n_trials': r['n_trials'], 'n_units': r['n_units']}\n    66→    for r in results\n    67→],\n    68→```\n    69→\n    70→iii. The dandiset already stores one session per file, so the file boundary is the session boundary and nothing has to be inferred. Because the filename embeds the acquisition timestamp (`sub-440956_ses-20190207T120657_...`), sorting the paths puts sessions in chronological order within each subject. 173 of the 174 files reach the output; the one exception is dropped for having no quality-controlled units (see 2-c).\n    71→\n    72→## 1-d. Are the data correctly split into trials?\n    73→\n    74→i. Trials come from the NWB trials table (`nwb.trials`), one row per behavioural trial, with exactly one go-cue event per row. The row count is checked against the number of go-cue events.\n    75→\n    76→ii.\n    77→```python\n    78→trials = nwb.trials.to_dataframe()\n    79→go = np.asarray(bev['go_start_times'].timestamps)\n    80→assert len(go) == len(trials), f'{len(go)} go cues vs {len(trials)} trials'\n    81→```\n    82→\n    83→iii. Trials are clearly defined by the trials table, so it is used directly rather than re-deriving boundaries from the event streams. `go_start_times` has exactly one event per trial in all 174 sessions, which makes the mapping unambiguous. This is not true of the other trial-phase events: `sample_start_times` and `delay_start_times` can have several entries per trial, because a lick during the sample or delay replays that epoch.\n    84→\n    85→## 1-e. How are trials filtered based on quality controls?\n    86→\n    87→i. Trials are filtered only for absence of spike data, in two cases. First, trials outside `units/obs_intervals`: in 8 sessions the ephys recording starts after the behaviour, leaving up to 376 leading trials with no spikes. Second, `free_water` trials, which have no spikes anywhere in the window even though `obs_intervals` covers them. A session is dropped entirely if fewer than 2 trials survive. No behavioural quality filter is applied.\n    88→\n    89→ii.\n    90→```python\n    91→# filter behavioral trials with no spike data\n    92→oi_off = np.asarray(units['obs_intervals'].data)\n    93→oi_start = np.concatenate([[0], oi_off[:-1]])\n    94→obs = np.asarray(units['obs_intervals'].target.data)[oi_start[good[0]]:oi_off[good[0]]]\n    95→keep = np.isin(np.round(trials['start_time'].values, 4), np.round(obs[:, 0], 4))\n    96→assert keep.sum() == len(obs), f'{keep.sum()} matched vs {len(obs)} observed'\n    97→\n    98→# filter no water trials\n    99→keep &= trials['free_water'].values == 0\n   100→if keep.sum() < 2:\n   101→    return None\n   102→trials = trials[keep]\n   103→go = go[keep]\n   104→```\n   105→\n   106→iii. `obs_intervals` is the file's own record of which trials were observed, and its start times match `trials.start_time`, which makes the mapping exact. The `free_water` exclusion was found empirically — those trials are covered by `obs_intervals` yet contain no spikes at all — and accounts for 2,449 of the 2,451 all-zero trials remaining after the `obs_intervals` filter.\n   107→\n   108→Early-lick and no-response (`ignore`) trials are deliberately kept, even though the data paper states \"Early lick trials and no response trials were excluded for analysis\", because both are required decoder outputs here. The 2-trial minimum is the target format's requirement.\n   109→\n   110→Together these remove 3,510 of the 94,370 trials in the retained sessions (3.7%), leaving 90,860.\n   111→\n   112→## 2-a. What variables in the raw data is the final `neural` data derived from?\n   113→\n   114→i. Neural data is derived from `units/spike_times`, the sorted spike times of each unit in session-absolute seconds. Only units with `classification == 'good'` contribute (see 2-c). The go-cue times (`BehavioralEvents/go_start_times`) are the other input, used to place the bin edges.\n   115→\n   116→ii.\n   117→```python\n   118→offs = np.asarray(units['spike_times'].data)\n   119→allst = np.asarray(units['spike_times'].target.data)\n   120→starts = np.concatenate([[0], offs[:-1]])\n   121→\n   122→edges = (go[:, None] + REL_EDGES[None, :]).ravel()\n   123→rates = np.empty((good.size, n_trials, N_BINS), np.float32)\n   124→for r, u in enumerate(good):\n   125→    s = allst[starts[u]:offs[u]]\n   126→```\n   127→\n   128→iii. `spike_times` is the only neural representation in the file, so firing rates are computed from it directly.\n   129→\n   130→## 2-b. How is the `neural` data processed?\n   131→\n   132→i. Spike times are converted to per-bin firing rates in Hz. For each good unit, the bin edges for every trial are built as one flat array of absolute times, `np.searchsorted` gives the running spike count at each edge, and differencing adjacent counts gives the spike count per bin. Counts are divided by the bin width to give Hz. No smoothing, normalisation, or baseline subtraction is applied.\n   133→\n   134→ii.\n   135→```python\n   136→edges = (go[:, None] + REL_EDGES[None, :]).ravel()\n   137→rates = np.empty((good.size, n_trials, N_BINS), np.float32)\n   138→for r, u in enumerate(good):\n   139→    s = allst[starts[u]:offs[u]]\n   140→    # running spike total at each edge; differencing gives the count per bin\n   141→    pos = np.searchsorted(s, edges).reshape(n_trials, N_BINS + 1)\n   142→    rates[r] = np.diff(pos, axis=1)\n   143→rates /= BIN                                  # counts -> Hz\n   144→```\n   145→\n   146→iii. Firing rate is computed from the binned spike count over the 50 ms window defined in the instructions. This also matches the reference code, which calls `sliding_histogram(..., rate=True)` and returns `binSpikes / bin_width`.\n   147→\n   148→## 2-c. How is the `neural` data filtered based on quality controls?\n   149→\n   150→i. Only units with `units/classification == 'good'` are kept. No thresholds are applied to any individual quality metric. A session with no such units is dropped entirely. This retains 69,453 of 272,227 units (25.5%), a median of 390 per session.\n   151→\n   152→ii.\n   153→```python\n   154→# ---- units: QC classifier verdict ----------------------------------\n   155→cls = _text(units['classification'])\n   156→good = np.flatnonzero(cls == 'good')\n   157→if good.size == 0:\n   158→    return None\n   159→```\n   160→\n   161→iii. `classification` is the verdict of the spike-sorting quality-control classifier described in `ChenLiuEtAl2023_SpikeSortingQC.pdf`.\n   162→\n   163→`units/unit_quality` ('good' / 'multi') is deliberately not used: it is an older label that disagrees on 12.2% of the classifier-good units and is far more permissive.\n   164→\n   165→The one dropped session (`sub-440958_ses-20190216T162508`) has `classification` and `anno_name` set to NaN for all 1,852 units, i.e. it was never quality-controlled. Excluding it gives 173 sessions and 69,453 good units, against the white paper's 173 sessions and 69,943 units.\n   166→\n   167→## 2-d. How is the per-trial `neural` data aligned to the event described in the `instructions`?\n   168→\n   169→i. Spike times and event times are already on the same session-absolute clock, so no alignment step is needed. The bin edges relative to the go cue are added to each trial's go-cue time to give the absolute time window for that trial, and the spikes are binned against those edges directly.\n   170→\n   171→ii.\n   172→```python\n   173→go = np.asarray(bev['go_start_times'].timestamps)\n   174→```\n   175→\n   176→```python\n   177→edges = (go[:, None] + REL_EDGES[None, :]).ravel()\n   178→rates = np.empty((good.size, n_trials, N_BINS), np.float32)\n   179→for r, u in enumerate(good):\n   180→    s = allst[starts[u]:offs[u]]\n   181→    pos = np.searchsorted(s, edges).reshape(n_trials, N_BINS + 1)\n   182→    rates[r] = np.diff(pos, axis=1)\n   183→```\n   184→\n   185→iii. Everything in the NWB file is timestamped on one global clock, so aligning to the go cue only requires looking up each trial's go-cue time and taking the window around it. There is no resampling or interpolation, and no per-stream offset to correct.\n   186→\n   187→## 2-e. How is the `neural` data temporally binned/resampled?\n   188→\n   189→i. Spike times are binned into 80 non-overlapping 50 ms bins spanning -2.5 s to +1.5 s relative to the go cue. The bin grid is defined once, as 81 edges relative to the go cue, and reused for every trial and session, so every trial has the same 80 timepoints.\n   190→\n   191→ii.\n   192→```python\n   193→# trial window relative to the go cue, and bin width, in seconds\n   194→T_START, T_STOP = -2.5, 1.5\n   195→BIN = 0.05\n   196→N_BINS = int(round((T_STOP - T_START) / BIN))             \n   197→\n   198→REL_EDGES = T_START + BIN * np.arange(N_BINS + 1)         \n   199→CENTERS = REL_EDGES[:-1] + BIN / 2                        \n   200→```\n   201→\n   202→```python\n   203→edges = (go[:, None] + REL_EDGES[None, :]).ravel()\n   204→```\n   205→\n   206→iii. The window and the 50 ms bin width are set by the instructions. Defining the grid once as offsets from the go cue gives a constant 80 timepoints per trial, which the target format requires.\n   207→\n   208→## 3-a. What variables in the raw data is `input` *time_from_tone_onset* derived from?\n   209→\n   210→i. From `sample_start_times`, the tone onsets of the session, together with the go cue of each trial. The tone taken for a trial is the **last one** before its go cue.\n   211→\n   212→ii. The tone of each trial:\n   213→```python\n   214→sample = np.asarray(bev['sample_start_times'].timestamps)\n   215→tone = sample[np.searchsorted(sample, go, side='left') - 1]\n   216→```\n   217→\n   218→iii. An early lick replays the sample epoch, so a trial can carry more than one tone; the last one before the go cue is the one the animal actually used.\n   219→\n   220→## 3-b. What processing is involved in computing `input` *time_from_tone_onset*?\n   221→\n   222→i. The bins sit around the go cue, so the value of a bin is its center plus the gap between the tone and the go cue.\n   223→\n   224→ii. The bin center, and the shift that turns them into time from the tone:\n   225→```python\n   226→CENTERS = REL_EDGES[:-1] + BIN / 2                        \n   227→```\n   228→```python\n   229→# bins sit around the go cue, values are seconds since the tone\n   230→time_from_tone = CENTERS[None, :] + (go - tone)[:, None]\n   231→```\n   232→\n   233→iii. No additional processing needed other than find the tone onset time.\n   234→\n   235→## 3-c. How is `input` *time_from_tone_onset* aligned with the neural data?\n   236→\n   237→i. It is how the neural binnning grid is defined.\n   238→\n   239→ii. The grid, defined once as offsets from the go cue, and laid on each trial's go cue to bin the spikes:\n   240→```python\n   241→T_START, T_STOP = -2.5, 1.5\n   242→BIN = 0.05\n   243→N_BINS = int(round((T_STOP - T_START) / BIN))             \n   244→\n   245→REL_EDGES = T_START + BIN * np.arange(N_BINS + 1)         \n   246→CENTERS = REL_EDGES[:-1] + BIN / 2                        \n   247→```\n   248→```python\n   249→edges = (go[:, None] + REL_EDGES[None, :]).ravel()\n   250→```\n   251→\n   252→iii. N/A\n   253→\n   254→## 4-a. What variables in the raw data is `input` *photostim* derived from?\n   255→\n   256→i. From `photostim_onset` and `photostim_duration` in the trials table, with `start_time` and the go cue used to place them on the trial's time axis.\n   257→\n   258→ii. The onset and offset of the stimulation, relative to the go cue:\n   259→```python\n   260→on_s = trials['photostim_onset'].values\n   261→has = on_s != 'N/A'\n   262→stim_on[has] = trials['start_time'].values[has] + on_s[has].astype(float) - go[has]\n   263→stim_off[has] = stim_on[has] + trials['photostim_duration'].values[has].astype(float)\n   264→```\n   265→\n   266→iii. The onsets are stored as strings measured from trial start, with `'N/A'` on the trials that were not stimulated, so they have to be converted and re-expressed against the go cue the bins are aligned on.\n   267→\n   268→## 4-b. What processing is involved in computing `input` *photostim*?\n   269→\n   270→i. A bin is 1 where its center falls between the onset and the offset of the stimulation and 0 elsewhere, so the input is a binary time series rather than a per trial flag.\n   271→\n   272→ii. The bins the light is on for:\n   273→```python\n   274→# NaN compares False, so non-stim trials stay 0\n   275→photostim = ((CENTERS[None, :] >= stim_on[:, None])\n   276→             & (CENTERS[None, :] < stim_off[:, None]))\n   277→```\n   278→\n   279→iii. A trial without stimulation keeps NaN bounds, and NaN compares false, so all of its bins come out 0 without a separate branch.\n   280→\n   281→## 4-c. How is `input` *photostim* aligned with the neural data?\n   282→\n   283→i. The onset and offset are expressed relative to the go cue, which is the event the neural bins are aligned on, so they can be compared against the bin center directly.\n   284→\n   285→ii. The onset put on the same axis as the bins:\n   286→```python\n   287→stim_on[has] = trials['start_time'].values[has] + on_s[has].astype(float) - go[has]\n   288→```\n   289→\n   290→iii. N/A\n   291→\n   292→## 5-a. What variables in the raw data is `output` *choice* derived from?\n   293→\n   294→i. There is no choice column in the file. Choice is derived from two trials-table columns: `trial_instruction` (`'left'` / `'right'`, the side the tone instructed) and `outcome` (`'hit'` / `'miss'` / `'ignore'`). A hit means the animal licked the instructed side, a miss means it licked the other side, and an `ignore` means it never licked.\n   295→\n   296→ii.\n   297→```python\n   298→SIDE_CODE = {'left': 0, 'right': 1}\n   299→CHOICE_NO_LICK = 2               # no lick in the response window\n   300→```\n   301→\n   302→```python\n   303→# choice is not stored; derive it from instruction x outcome\n   304→outcome_s = trials['outcome'].values\n   305→side = np.array([SIDE_CODE[x] for x in trials['trial_instruction'].values])\n   306→choice = np.where(outcome_s == 'ignore', CHOICE_NO_LICK,\n   307→                  np.where(outcome_s == 'hit', side, 1 - side))\n   308→```\n   309→\n   310→iii. The animal's actual lick direction is not stored, but it is fully determined by the instructed side and the outcome, so it is derived from those two columns. `outcome == 'ignore'` was verified to mean no lick anywhere in `[go, go + 1.5]`, with no exceptions, so choice is genuinely undefined on those trials and gets its own third value.\n   311→\n   312→## 5-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *choice*?\n   313→\n   314→i. The derived choice is coded as `0` left, `1` right, `2` no lick, and written into row 0 of the per-trial output array, repeated across all 80 bins. `output_values[0]` names the three codes.\n   315→\n   316→ii.\n   317→```python\n   318→SIDE_CODE = {'left': 0, 'right': 1}\n   319→CHOICE_NO_LICK = 2               # no lick in the response window\n   320→\n   321→OUTPUT_VALUES = [\n   322→    ['left', 'right', 'no lick'],\n   323→    ...\n   324→]\n   325→```\n   326→\n   327→```python\n   328→# choice is not stored; derive it from instruction x outcome\n   329→outcome_s = trials['outcome'].values\n   330→side = np.array([SIDE_CODE[x] for x in trials['trial_instruction'].values])\n   331→choice = np.where(outcome_s == 'ignore', CHOICE_NO_LICK,\n   332→                  np.where(outcome_s == 'hit', side, 1 - side))\n   333→\n   334→# per-trial values are repeated across bins so all outputs share one array\n   335→out = np.empty((n_trials, len(OUTPUT_NAMES), N_BINS), np.int8)\n   336→out[:, 0, :] = choice[:, None]\n   337→```\n   338→\n   339→iii. `left = 0` and `right = 1` follow the instructions, and a third class is defined for the no-lick case. Choice is one value per trial, so it is repeated across the 80 bins to keep all four outputs in a single `(n_output, n_timepoints)` array.\n   340→\n   341→## 6-a. What variables in the raw data is `output` *outcome* derived from?\n   342→\n   343→i. Outcome comes directly from the `outcome` column of the trials table, which already holds the strings `'ignore'`, `'miss'`, and `'hit'`.\n   344→\n   345→ii.\n   346→```python\n   347→outcome_s = trials['outcome'].values\n   348→```\n   349→\n   350→iii. The trials table stores the outcome explicitly with exactly the three categories the instructions ask for, so no derivation is needed.\n   351→\n   352→## 6-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *outcome*?\n   353→\n   354→i. The three strings are mapped to `0` ignore, `1` miss, `2` hit via a fixed dictionary, and written into row 1 of the output array, repeated across all 80 bins.\n   355→\n   356→ii.\n   357→```python\n   358→OUTCOME_CODE = {'ignore': 0, 'miss': 1, 'hit': 2}\n   359→```\n   360→\n   361→```python\n   362→out[:, 1, :] = np.array([OUTCOME_CODE[x] for x in outcome_s])[:, None]\n   363→```\n   364→\n   365→iii. The code assignment follows the instructions. Outcome is one value per trial, so it is repeated across bins like the other per-trial outputs.\n   366→\n   367→## 7-a. What variables in the raw data is `output` *early_lick* derived from?\n   368→\n   369→i. From the `early_lick` column of the trials table, which holds the strings `'no early'` and `'early'`.\n   370→\n   371→ii.\n   372→```python\n   373→trials['early_lick'].values\n   374→```\n   375→\n   376→iii. The trials table flags early licking explicitly, so no derivation is needed. The lick that sets the flag occurs during the sample or delay epoch, before the go cue, so the event itself falls inside the -2.5 s window even though the flag is stored per trial.\n   377→\n   378→## 7-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *early_lick*?\n   379→\n   380→i. The two strings are mapped to `0` no, `1` yes via a fixed dictionary, and written into row 2 of the output array, repeated across all 80 bins.\n   381→\n   382→ii.\n   383→```python\n   384→EARLY_CODE = {'no early': 0, 'early': 1}\n   385→```\n   386→\n   387→```python\n   388→out[:, 2, :] = np.array([EARLY_CODE[x] for x in trials['early_lick'].values])[:, None]\n   389→```\n   390→\n   391→iii. The code assignment follows the instructions. One value per trial, so repeated across bins like the other per-trial outputs.\n   392→\n   393→## 8-a. What variables in the raw data is `output` *tongue_y_position* derived from?\n   394→\n   395→i. From `acquisition/BehavioralTimeSeries/Camera0_side_TongueTracking`, whose `data` is `(n_frames, 3)` = `tongue_x`, `tongue_y`, `tongue_likelihood`, with matching `timestamps`. Column 1 (`tongue_y`) is the value; column 2 (`likelihood`) decides whether the tongue is visible in that frame.\n   396→\n   397→ii.\n   398→```python\n   399→ts = nwb.acquisition['BehavioralTimeSeries'].time_series['Camera0_side_TongueTracking']\n   400→t_cam = np.asarray(ts.timestamps)\n   401→data = ts.data[:]                        # (n_frames, 3) = x, y, likelihood\n   402→y = data[:, 1].copy()\n   403→```\n   404→\n   405→iii. This is the only tongue measurement in the file. The channel layout is stated in the series' own `description` attribute (`\"('tongue_x', 'tongue_y', 'tongue_likelihood')\"`) rather than assumed. Tracking is present in all 174 sessions and runs at ~294 Hz.\n   406→\n   407→## 8-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *tongue_y_position*?\n   408→\n   409→i. Four steps. Frames with `likelihood < 0.5` are set to NaN, since the tracker still reports a position when the tongue is not protruding. The surviving values are averaged into 50 ms bins across the whole session, and the 40th and 60th percentiles of *those bin means* give two class edges. Each trial's window is then binned the same way and digitised against those edges, giving classes 0/1/2. Bins containing no visible frame are assigned a fourth class, `3` (`'not visible'`).\n   410→\n   411→ii.\n   412→```python\n   413→TONGUE_CONF = 0.5                # tracking likelihood for the tongue to count as visible\n   414→TONGUE_PCT = (40, 60)            # per-session percentiles -> 3 visible classes\n   415→TONGUE_HIDDEN = 3                # no visible tongue frame in the bin\n   416→```\n   417→\n   418→```python\n   419→y[data[:, 2] < TONGUE_CONF] = np.nan     # keep only visible frames\n   420→\n   421→# bin the whole session on one grid, then take percentiles of the bin means\n   422→gidx = np.floor((t_cam - t_cam[0]) / BIN).astype(np.int64)\n   423→edges = np.nanpercentile(_bin_mean(gidx, y, int(gidx[-1]) + 1), TONGUE_PCT)\n   424→\n   425→# per trial, bin the same way and digitise against those edges\n   426→cls = np.full((len(go), N_BINS), TONGUE_HIDDEN, np.int8)\n   427→lo = np.searchsorted(t_cam, go + T_START, 'left')\n   428→hi = np.searchsorted(t_cam, go + T_STOP, 'left')\n   429→for i in range(len(go)):\n   430→    b = np.floor((t_cam[lo[i]:hi[i]] - (go[i] + T_START)) / BIN).astype(np.int64)\n   431→    np.clip(b, 0, N_BINS - 1, out=b)\n   432→    m = _bin_mean(b, y[lo[i]:hi[i]], N_BINS)\n   433→    ok = ~np.isnan(m)\n   434→    cls[i, ok] = np.digitize(m[ok], edges)\n   435→```\n   436→\n   437→iii. The tongue is visible in only ~10% of frames, and when it is retracted the tracker still emits a position, so those frames are discarded rather than averaged in — including them shifts the percentiles and mixes real protrusions with noise. The likelihood value is effectively binary (89% of frames below 0.01, 10.5% at or above 0.99), so the exact threshold does not matter.\n   438→\n   439→Percentiles are taken over the 50 ms bin means rather than raw frames so the edges are defined on the same quantity that gets discretised; taking them over frames instead skews the resulting class balance, because averaging within a bin pulls values toward the center. The 40th/60th split and the per-session scope both follow the instructions. A fourth class is defined for bins with no visible tongue, which is 75% of all bins.\n   440→\n   441→## 8-d. How is `output` *tongue_y_position* aligned with the neural data?\n   442→\n   443→i. This is the one genuinely time-varying output, so it is the only one needing real alignment. The camera timestamps are on the same session-absolute clock as the spikes and the go cues, so each trial's frame range is found by `searchsorted` on the camera timestamps at `go + T_START` and `go + T_STOP`, and frames are assigned to bins by their offset from `go + T_START` — the same go-cue-relative grid used for the firing rates.\n   444→\n   445→ii.\n   446→```python\n   447→lo = np.searchsorted(t_cam, go + T_START, 'left')\n   448→hi = np.searchsorted(t_cam, go + T_STOP, 'left')\n   449→for i in range(len(go)):\n   450→    b = np.floor((t_cam[lo[i]:hi[i]] - (go[i] + T_START)) / BIN).astype(np.int64)\n   451→```\n   452→\n   453→iii. The camera timestamps share the global clock with the spikes and events, so no interpolation or offset correction is needed — the same bin grid is applied to both streams, which guarantees bin `k` of the tongue output covers the same interval as bin `k` of the firing rates.\n   454→\n   455→Unlike the spikes, the video is trial-gated: it runs from `trials.start_time` to the trial-end event and is off during the inter-trial interval. On the ~3% of trials where the go cue falls less than 2.5 s after trial start, the leading bins therefore contain no frames at all and fall into the `'not visible'` class.\n   456→\n   457→## 9. How are minor mistakes in the data, e.g. missing data, handled?\n   458→\n   459→i. Three cases:\n   460→\n   461→- **Session never quality-controlled**: `classification` and `anno_name` are NaN rather than strings. `_text` maps any non-string entry to `''`, the session then has no `'good'` units, and it is dropped.\n   462→- **Trials without spike data**: excluded via `obs_intervals` and `free_water` (see 1-e).\n   463→- **Frames with no tracked tongue**: set to NaN and excluded from the bin mean; a bin left with no valid frame becomes the `'not visible'` class.\n   464→\n   465→ii.\n   466→```python\n   467→def _text(col):\n   468→    \"\"\"Read a text column; non-str entries (unlabelled sessions) become ''.\"\"\"\n   469→    return np.array([x if isinstance(x, str) else '' for x in col[:]])\n   470→```\n   471→\n   472→```python\n   473→good = np.flatnonzero(cls == 'good')\n   474→if good.size == 0:\n   475→    return None\n   476→```\n   477→\n   478→```python\n   479→y[data[:, 2] < TONGUE_CONF] = np.nan     # keep only visible frames\n   480→...\n   481→m = _bin_mean(b, y[lo[i]:hi[i]], N_BINS)\n   482→ok = ~np.isnan(m)\n   483→cls[i, ok] = np.digitize(m[ok], edges)\n   484→```\n   485→\n   486→iii. Missing data is handled one of two ways depending on what it means. Where nothing was recorded, the session or trial is excluded, since emitting it would fabricate data — a trial with no spikes would otherwise appear as 4 s of 0 Hz across every unit. Where the measurement legitimately has no value, as with a retracted tongue, it is represented as an explicit category rather than imputed.\n   487→\n   488→## 10-a. What are the most time-consuming steps of the code?\n   489→\n   490→i. Reading each NWB file dominates. The full conversion takes 247 s for 174 sessions, ~1.4 s per session on average, ranging from ~0.7 s to ~4 s roughly with unit count. Within a session the costs are pulling the `spike_times` buffer (up to ~11.5 M doubles) and the tongue tracking array (~680 k x 3), then the per-unit `searchsorted` loop. Pickling the 11.9 GB result takes a further ~40 s.\n   491→\n   492→ii. N/A\n   493→\n   494→iii. The work is dominated by I/O and by one binary search per bin edge per unit, both of which scale with the data actually needed. No step was worth optimising further given the 15-minute budget in the instructions.\n   495→\n   496→## 10-b. What loops in the code could have been vectorized to improve efficiency?\n   497→\n   498→i. Two loops remain. The per-unit loop in the neural binning runs one `searchsorted` per unit, but over *all* trials at once — the per-trial dimension is already vectorised by flattening the edge array. The per-trial loop in `tongue_output` bins one trial's frames at a time.\n   499→\n   500→ii.\n   501→```python\n   502→edges = (go[:, None] + REL_EDGES[None, :]).ravel()\n   503→for r, u in enumerate(good):\n   504→    s = allst[starts[u]:offs[u]]\n   505→    pos = np.searchsorted(s, edges).reshape(n_trials, N_BINS + 1)\n   506→```\n   507→\n   508→iii. The per-unit loop cannot be collapsed further because each unit has a different number of spikes, so there is no single sorted array to search — this is inherent to the ragged storage. The tongue loop could be vectorised with a global bin index and one `bincount`, but it runs over trials rather than frames and is not a measurable share of runtime, so it was left in the clearer form.\n   509→\n   510→## 10-c. What processing does the code repeat multiple times?\n   511→\n   512→i. Nothing is recomputed. Each NWB file is opened once and every quantity derived from it is computed once. The bin grid (`REL_EDGES`, `CENTERS`) is built once at module level and reused for every trial and session.\n   513→\n   514→ii. N/A\n   515→\n   516→iii. The conversion is a single pass over the files. Because the tongue discretisation edges are per-session rather than global, they can be computed inside that same pass — no second pass over the data is needed to establish them.\n   517→\n   518→## 10-d. What unnecessary processing does the code do that is discarded in downstream analyses?\n   519→\n   520→i. None. Every field computed goes into the output.\n   521→\n   522→ii. N/A\n   523→\n   524→iii. N/A\n   525→\n   526→## 10-e. How is memory usage optimized?\n   527→\n   528→i. Firing rates are stored as `float32` and the outputs as `int8` rather than the default `float64`. Each session is read inside a `with` block so the file handle and its cached arrays are released on exit, and only the per-trial slices are retained. The full neural payload is 11.9 GB; a single session's working arrays are at most ~0.2 GB.\n   529→\n   530→ii.\n   531→```python\n   532→rates = np.empty((good.size, n_trials, N_BINS), np.float32)\n   533→```\n   534→\n   535→```python\n   536→out = np.empty((n_trials, len(OUTPUT_NAMES), N_BINS), np.int8)\n   537→```\n   538→\n   539→```python\n   540→inp = np.stack([time_from_tone, photostim], axis=1).astype(np.float32)\n   541→```\n   542→\n   543→iii. `float32` halves the largest object in the output relative to `float64` at no cost, since firing rates are multiples of 20 Hz and well within its precision. Outputs are small non-negative integers, so `int8` is sufficient. Peak memory is set by the accumulated result rather than by any single session, so no further streaming was needed.\n   544→"}]},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"170f72c4-c802-4ae4-893f-74b71c56ed17","uuid":"5eb7075c-feea-47b9-afc7-78f66a90a762","timestamp":"2026-09-23T18:22:10.218Z","tool_use_result":{"type":"text","file":{"filePath":"/tests/reference_DECISIONS.md","content":"# Decisions\n\n## 1-a. How are **all the data** for all subjects, sessions, and trials loaded in?\n\ni. The dataset is distributed as one NWB file per session under `data/sub-<subject_id>/`. All sessions are found with a single glob over that layout, and each file is opened with `pynwb` and processed once. Subjects, trials, and units are then read from within each file (`nwb.subject`, `nwb.trials`, `nwb.units`, `nwb.acquisition`).\n\nii.
